 2 11:35:06                Presentation by MR FALKENSTERN
 3 11:35:07    WITNESS:  Good morning.  My name is David Falkenstern,
 4 11:35:13       and I'm the claimant's delay and disruption expert.
 5 11:35:16           For more than 20 years, my work has focused on
 6 11:35:18       providing independent expert evidence in the fields of
 7 11:35:22       delay and disruption.
 8 11:35:23           More recently, I founded a boutique expert witness
 9 11:35:26       firm specialising in delay and project management
10 11:35:29       expert evidence.
11 11:35:30           I co-chair the UK Society of Construction Law, and
12 11:35:34       I chair the drafting committee of the SCL Delay and
13 11:35:38       Disruption Protocol.
14 11:35:41           For the next 30 minutes or so, I'll take the
15 11:35:44       tribunal through my opinions.
16 11:35:46           I'll begin with my analysis of the delays to each
17 11:35:49       of the five addendum milestones before turning to my
18 11:35:53       disruption analyses for the 14 work categories
19 11:35:56       addressed by Mr Merrett in his first report.
20 11:35:59           Throughout this presentation, I will refer to the
21 11:36:01       reports listed on this slide.  Of those documents, the
22 11:36:05       second delay joint statement provides the clearest
23 11:36:08       summary of the experts' final delay and disruption
24 11:36:11       opinions.
25 11:36:14           First, on slide 3, and before addressing my delay
                                      44
 1 11:36:17       analysis for each milestone, I will address several
 2 11:36:20       overarching criticisms made of my approach.
 3 11:36:25           First, it has been suggested that my analysis
 4 11:36:28       assumes DTC could simply move resources from one part
 5 11:36:31       of the site to another on a daily basis, to work
 6 11:36:35       around obstructions.  That is not my opinion, nor is
 7 11:36:39       it something I've stated in my reports.
 8 11:36:44           Mr Merrett suggests that simply because DTC
 9 11:36:47       recorded work fronts as available, it does not follow
10 11:36:49       that there is no obstructions, clashes or other
11 11:36:53       restrictions.
12 11:36:54           As a general proposition, that may be true.
13 11:36:57       However, there were contemporaneous records prepared
14 11:37:00       specifically to record work front availability and
15 11:37:03       restrictions.  Mr Merrett has not identified any
16 11:37:08       reliable basis for disregarding those records on the
17 11:37:11       occasions where they do not support his opinions.
18 11:37:15           It has been suggested that my approach is flawed
19 11:37:18       because I analysed the period before addendum 4.
20 11:37:20       I disagree.  The analysis is necessary to establish
21 11:37:25       the extent of delay during the post-addendum 4 period.
22 11:37:28           However, I accept that the causes of delay before
23 11:37:31       addendum 4 may not be relevant to the issues in
24 11:37:33       dispute between the parties.
25 11:37:36           Accordingly, I will not address that period during
                                      45
 1 11:37:38       my presentation.
 2 11:37:42           Mr Merrett further suggests that my opinion
 3 11:37:44       regarding descoping mitigation is incorrect because
 4 11:37:48       delay accumulates while critical activities are
 5 11:37:50       delayed.
 6 11:37:51           I agree that delay accumulates.  However, when
 7 11:37:55       significant portions of the critical scope are
 8 11:37:57       removed, the extent of accumulated delay will
 9 11:38:00       decrease.
10 11:38:01           For the purposes of this presentation, I will
11 11:38:04       focus on the causes of delay and will not address
12 11:38:07       descoping mitigation further.
13 11:38:11           Mr Merrett also states that my analysis is
14 11:38:14       inconsistent with the principles set out in the SCL
15 11:38:16       Delay and Disruption Protocol.  In relation to
16 11:38:20       criticality and delay assessment, I disagree.
17 11:38:22           The protocol explains that criticality should be
18 11:38:25       determined through a common sense and practical
19 11:38:28       analysis of the available facts, and should not depend
20 11:38:31       substantially on programming software alone.  My
21 11:38:35       analysis follows that approach.
22 11:38:38           Similarly, the protocol explains that delay should
23 11:38:39       be assessed by comparing actual progress along the
24 11:38:42       contemporaneous critical path with the corresponding
25 11:38:45       planned dates.  That is the methodology adopted
                                      46
 1 11:38:48       throughout my analysis.
 2 11:38:52           Finally, Mr Merrett suggests that criticality
 3 11:38:54       should be determined primarily by the programme,
 4 11:38:56       rather than the extent of remaining works.
 5 11:38:59           In my opinion, that approach places undue emphasis
 6 11:39:02       on erroneous programmes and is inconsistent with the
 7 11:39:05       protocol.
 8 11:39:07           With those points addressed, I will now turn to
 9 11:39:09       the individual milestones.
10 11:39:12           Slide 4 sets out my analysis of milestone 5.
11 11:39:15           I will begin with milestone 5 because it concerns
12 11:39:17       the completion of the works as a whole, with the
13 11:39:20       remaining milestones effectively forming subsets of
14 11:39:24       that overall scope.
15 11:39:26           For each milestone, I will identify the location
16 11:39:28       of the associated scope of works by highlighting the
17 11:39:30       relevant work areas.
18 11:39:32           As shown, the scope of milestone 5 comprises of
19 11:39:35       DTC's entire scope of work within 18 units across five
20 11:39:39       separate lots.
21 11:39:42           I will also show for each milestone the extent of
22 11:39:44       progress achieved for the activities within the
23 11:39:46       milestone scope.
24 11:39:48           For activities that progressed, I charted the
25 11:39:50       overall per cent complete achieved during the analysis
                                      47
 1 11:39:53       period.  Activities that made no progress are listed
 2 11:39:56       separately on the right-hand side of the chart.
 3 11:40:00           Milestone 5 works were planned to be complete by
 4 11:40:05       31 August 2023.  In fact, none of the work fronts
 5 11:40:08       achieved that completion date.
 6 11:40:10           During the nine-month period following addendum 4
 7 11:40:12       and prior to termination, only limited gravity pipe,
 8 11:40:16       structural concrete, grouting, cable trench and paving
 9 11:40:19       works progressed, all of which are shown on the chart.
10 11:40:23           No progress was made on the asphalt roads, gravel
11 11:40:26       finishing, excavation, embankment and demolition
12 11:40:29       works, which are listed on the right-hand side of the
13 11:40:32       chart.
14 11:40:33           I calculated 236 days of critical delay during the
15 11:40:37       269-day post-addendum 4 period.
16 11:40:42           Despite that limited progress, DTC maintained a
17 11:40:45       substantial workforce on site, with actual manpower
18 11:40:48       levels, shown in blue, remaining close to the
19 11:40:50       originally planned levels, shown in green.
20 11:40:55           Given the absence of measurable progress, the
21 11:40:57       available evidence does not establish what those
22 11:40:59       resources were doing.
23 11:41:02           The contemporaneous evidence establishes only
24 11:41:04       four days of delay attributable to strikes.  The
25 11:41:08       remaining 232 days of critical delay are unexplained.
                                      48
 1 11:41:12       The delay events relied upon by Mr Merrett do not
 2 11:41:15       account for those 232 days.
 3 11:41:20           For example, up until 24 August 2023, which marks
 4 11:41:24       the end of Mr Merritt's window 4, he attributes delay
 5 11:41:27       to two events.  These include a 32-day delay and
 6 11:41:31       a 15-day delay affecting the asphalt road works, said
 7 11:41:35       to arise from material shortages and isolated
 8 11:41:39       obstructions.
 9 11:41:40           Those events do not explain the complete absence
10 11:41:43       of asphalt road progress during the period.
11 11:41:46       Contemporaneous DTC records state that the asphalt
12 11:41:49       road works were 100 per cent available, indicating
13 11:41:52       that those issues were not materially constraining
14 11:41:55       progress.
15 11:41:56           Mr Merrett did not identify any contemporaneous
16 11:41:59       evidence demonstrating that either obstructions or
17 11:42:02       material supply issues prevented any asphalt road
18 11:42:05       progress.
19 11:42:08           Mr Merrett further concludes that 50 days of delay
20 11:42:10       after 24 August 2023 were caused primarily by external
21 11:42:15       access and area clearance issues affecting unit 51
22 11:42:18       trenches.
23 11:42:19           However, after 24 August 2023, the 2,301 linear
24 11:42:24       metres of unit 51 cable trenches were recorded by DTC
25 11:42:28       as available, with no restrictions identified and no
                                      49
 1 11:42:32       installation progress achieved.
 2 11:42:34           In any event, Mr Merrett did not identify
 3 11:42:37       contemporaneous evidence supporting those opinions.
 4 11:42:41           Finally, Mr Merrett concludes that three days of
 5 11:42:44       delay arose from descoping of the unit 5 asphalt road
 6 11:42:47       works.  It is unclear how descoping of works that had
 7 11:42:51       never progressed could itself have caused delay.
 8 11:42:57           Slide 5 sets out my analysis of milestone 1.
 9 11:43:01           The milestone primarily concerned the asphalt road
10 11:43:03       and cable trench works in units 61 and 907 within
11 11:43:08       lot 1.
12 11:43:09           The milestone 1 works were planned to be completed
13 11:43:12       by 31 May 2023.
14 11:43:15           In practice, progress during the post-addendum 4
15 11:43:18       period was minimal, as reflected by the near-flat
16 11:43:22       progress curves shown in the chart.

18 11:43:29       during the 269-day period prior to termination.
19 11:43:33           Despite that lack of progress, DTC expended more
20 11:43:36       man hours, as shown by the blue curve, than what was

 6 15:35:39       complete for a basket of activities.  And the reason
 7 15:35:44       why you have mitigation when you're descoping is
 8 15:35:47       because the overall extent to complete, or
 9 15:35:51       overall per cent complete, increases when you
10 15:35:55       descoping, because it's taking away the amount of work
11 15:35:59       you have left to do, that gives you a higher per cent
12 15:36:02       complete.
13 15:36:02           That's how the descoping works.  This is not a
14 15:36:08       critical path race between sequence 1 and sequence 2.
15 15:36:11       The programmes have no sequences.  There is no
16 15:36:15       interconnecting logic.
17 15:36:16           There are 207 work fronts with no logic between
18 15:36:21       them.  So you can't -- you don't have a sequence A or
19 15:36:25       B.  There are no sequences.

                                     195
 1 17:02:47           Thank you.
 2 17:02:50           In my opinion, the experts approach the same
 3 17:02:54       evidence through materially different lenses.
 4 17:02:57       Mr Falkenstern's analysis gives significant weight to
 5 17:03:00       the production level indicators, such as work fronts,
 6 17:03:02       remaining quantities, man hours and CPI.  This is not
 7 17:03:07       in accordance with the principles set out in the SCL
 8 17:03:08       Protocol in relation to an as-planned versus as-built
 9 17:03:13       assessment.
10 17:03:13           My analysis focuses on the activity sequence, the
11 17:03:17       relevant lot, the relevant window, and the
12 17:03:21       contemporaneous cause of delay or disruption in
13 17:03:23       accordance with the principles set out in the SCL
14 17:03:25       Protocol.
15 17:03:26           The next slide, please.
16 17:03:27           JS-1 is an important point of reference.
17 17:03:33       Mr Falkenstern and his predecessor, Ms Beauchamp,
18 17:03:37       agreed that the as-planned versus as-built windows
19 17:03:39       approach was the most appropriate method.  It was also
20 17:03:42       agreed, the SCL Protocol description of that method
21 17:03:45       and the definition of the critical path.
22 17:03:47           This issue is therefore not merely whether the
23 17:03:52       project had low progress, but what was planned, what
24 17:03:55       actually happened, which sequence control completion
25 17:03:58       and what caused the variance.
                                     196
 1 17:04:00           The agreed method is an effect cause approach in
 2 17:04:04       which the critical path is established first, and then
 3 17:04:07       the causes of delay are therefore identified, the
 4 17:04:09       focus of the longest sequence of activities driving
 5 17:04:12       the completion date in each window.
 6 17:04:14           If you go to the next slide, please.
 7 17:04:16           In my first report, I applied the framework by
 8 17:04:22       reference to the addendum 4 period, the relevant lots,
 9 17:04:25       the overall completion milestone, and the
10 17:04:27       contemporaneous records.
11 17:04:28           The point is important.  The analysis is not the
12 17:04:32       general criticism of the project productivity.  It is
13 17:04:36       a structured exercise using contemporaneous programme
14 17:04:39       updates, monthly and weekly progress records, daily
15 17:04:42       reports, correspondence, photographs and handover
16 17:04:46       evidence, to identify the controlling sequence and the
17 17:04:49       cause of delay.
18 17:04:49           In my assessment of each lot and the overall
19 17:04:52       assessment, identified the critical path and
20 17:04:54       calculated the extent of critical delay
21 17:04:56       retrospectively and each in accordance with the SCL
22 17:04:58       Protocol.
23 17:04:59           Go to the next slide, please.
24 17:05:01           I do not put my criticism as an allegation that
25 17:05:07       Mr Falkenstern carried out no as-planned versus
                                     197
 1 17:05:11       as-built analysis.  The more accurate point is that
 2 17:05:14       his application of the method materially departs from
 3 17:05:17       the agreed activity-based approach.
 4 17:05:19           Mr Falkenstern assesses a pre-addendum 4 period,
 5 17:05:22       uses a resource-driven view of criticality, relies on
 6 17:05:26       early dates and proxies, and leaves significant
 7 17:05:29       periods of residual categories, such as the balance of
 8 17:05:32       critical delay and effective suspension.
 9 17:05:35           In contrast, my analysis focuses on the period
10 17:05:39       after addendum 4 and identifies critical driving
11 17:05:43       sequence of activities and the causes of delay.
12 17:05:46       I found that delay was linked to issues such as
13 17:05:48       handover, obstructions, clashes with other
14 17:05:51       subcontractors, and the requirement to excavate by
15 17:05:54       hand, field engineering instructions, materials,
16 17:05:58       dewatering and other prerequisites.
17 17:06:01           Next slide, please.
18 17:06:02           The most important methodological issue is the
19 17:06:09       establishment of criticality.  A large remaining work
20 17:06:11       front is not automatically critical.  High man hours
21 17:06:14       and the large quantities may show scale or importance,
22 17:06:17       but they do not prove the longest controlling
23 17:06:21       sequence.
24 17:06:21           A smaller activity can be critical if it affects
25 17:06:23       downstream works.  Handover.  Backfilling.  Paving.
                                     198
 1 17:06:28       Completion.
 2 17:06:28           Essentially, the critical path is defined in the
 3 17:06:30       SCL Protocol as the longest sequence of activities
 4 17:06:35       through a project network from start to finish, the
 5 17:06:37       sum of whose durations determines the overall project
 6 17:06:40       duration.
 7 17:06:40           The key principle here is that seemingly smaller
 8 17:06:44       types of works can delay the project if their late
 9 17:06:46       finish dates are not met, and the delayed activity
10 17:06:49       affects other successor activities on the longest
11 17:06:53       path, as illustrated on this slide on the right-hand
12 17:06:55       side.
13 17:06:56           Go to the next slide, please.
14 17:06:59           There is also an important distinction between
15 17:07:04       slippage and critical delay.  Mr Falkenstern accepts
16 17:07:07       limitations in the programme data available to him,
17 17:07:10       including the absence of native Primavera information.
18 17:07:13           In my opinion, where late dates, float and logic
19 17:07:16       cannot be properly tested, for me it is unsafe to
20 17:07:20       convert delay under progress into critical delay
21 17:07:23       without a proper activity-based causation analysis.
22 17:07:26           The next slide, please.
23 17:07:28           Turning to the delay assessment itself, my first
24 17:07:34       report position is a constructive causation case.
25 17:07:37       Lot 1 is assessed at 232 days, lot 2 at 185 days,
                                     199
 1 17:07:42       lot 4 at 170 days and lot 5 at 100 days.
 2 17:07:47           These figures are not presented as general
 3 17:07:51       underperformance.  They are tied to specific units,
 4 17:07:54       activities, with their windows and their causes.
 5 17:07:55           The next slide, please.
 6 17:07:58           We're roughly about halfway through, gentlemen,
 7 17:08:02       just to let you know.
 8 17:08:03           Lot 1 illustrates the difference between the two
 9 17:08:08       approaches.  My assessment identifies moving lot 1
10 17:08:11       critical sequence through to unit 907 cable trench
11 17:08:15       excavation, unit 061 paving subgrade, unit 061 red
12 17:08:20       tiling cable trench completion, and then unit 061
13 17:08:25       asphalt-related works.
14 17:08:26           These are the activities that, in my opinion,
15 17:08:28       controlled the longest sequence of activities in each
16 17:08:31       of my windows.
17 17:08:31           Mr Falkenstern's analysis records substantial
18 17:08:35       delay, but leaves the principal periods as the balance
19 17:08:37       of delay or effective suspension.
20 17:08:40           Go to the next slide, please.

24 17:08:54       preparation and road completion activities.
25 17:08:56           The fact that an area existed somewhere on site
                                     200
 1 17:09:00       does not answer whether the predecessor works and
 2 17:09:02       handover is needed, the controlling sequence were
 3 17:09:05       complete, or that they were even critical.
 4 17:09:07           The next slide, please.
 5 17:09:11           Sorry, I've just missed my place.  Sorry.
 6 17:09:23           Lot 4 demonstrates why critical path may move.  My
 7 17:09:33       assessment identifies movement from unit 094 to
 8 17:09:36       unit 50 and then to unit 005.
 9 17:09:40           Unit 50 was affected by foundation modifications,
10 17:09:42       FEIs and delayed handovers.

14 17:09:55       remains capable of being identified in each window.
15 17:09:58           Again, Mr Falkenstern's establishment of the
16 17:10:00       critical path is not in accordance with the SCL
17 17:10:03       Protocol.
18 17:10:04           Next slide, please.
19 17:10:07           For lots 3 and 5, my concern is that the combined
20 17:10:14       addendum 4 milestone should not obscure unit-specific
21 17:10:16       causation.  Even where contractual milestone groups
22 17:10:20       lots 3 and 5, the cause of delay still needs to be
23 17:10:23       tested with the actual unit and activity affected.
24 17:10:26           Mr Falkenstern identified that lot 3 works were
25 17:10:30       completed prior to addendum 4, on the basis that
                                     201
 1 17:10:33       certain unit 56 works were removed from the programme
 2 17:10:37       WBS.  Should, however, the tribunal find this to be
 3 17:10:40       credible, my assessment will relate to lot 5 only.
 4 17:10:43       This, however, doesn't have any material impact on my
 5 17:10:47       assessment.
 6 17:10:47           Next slide, please.
 7 17:10:50           The overall completion analysis presents the same
 8 17:10:56       issue at the project level.  My analysis identifies
 9 17:10:59       the overall critical path as moving through units 94,
10 17:11:05       51, 005 in different windows.  My critical path and
11 17:11:09       delay calculations have been determined based on the
12 17:11:11       principles in the SCL Protocol.
13 17:11:12           Mr Falkenstern attributes only a small part of
14 17:11:16       delay to identified events, and leaves most of the
15 17:11:19       measured delay as a residual or unexplained by the
16 17:11:22       pleaded events.
17 17:11:22           In my opinion, this is a weak positive causation
18 17:11:26       case.
19 17:11:26           Next slide, please.
20 17:11:29           I now add an important consistency check from my
21 17:11:35       second report, section 7.
22 17:11:37           I do not treat the Bapco main works critical path
23 17:11:40       as the same thing as the subcontract critical path.
24 17:11:43       However, where the claimant alleges that the
25 17:11:47       respondent area 6 civil works delayed successor
                                     202
 1 17:11:50       activities or the wider project activities, the
 2 17:11:52       claimant's own progress reports to Bapco is relevant.
 3 17:11:56       It tests whether the wider allegation was being
 4 17:11:59       reported contemporaneously to the employer.
 5 17:12:01           Slide 16, next one, please.
 6 17:12:04           My review of the claimant's programme updates,
 7 17:12:09       dated 24th February, 31st March and 27th October, did
 8 17:12:14       not show the respondent's area 6 civils works as
 9 17:12:18       controlling the main works' critical path.  The
10 17:12:20       reported critical activities were instead principally
11 17:12:23       piping, hydrotesting, mechanical completion,
12 17:12:27       E&I testing, commissioning and the preparation for
13 17:12:32       RFSU and provisional acceptance certificates.
14 17:12:34           By 27 October 2023, the remaining area 6 civil
15 17:12:40       works were ongoing, but not shown as critical.
16 17:12:42           Next slide, please.
17 17:12:45           The distinction here is important.  Subcontract
18 17:12:53       criticality is the level relevant to addendum 4
19 17:12:56       milestones and what is the level principally addressed
20 17:12:58       in CM-1.
21 17:12:59           Main works criticality concerns what controlled
22 17:13:01       the claimant's completion to Bapco.
23 17:13:03           Interface or successor criticality arises only if
24 17:13:08       the claimant alleges the respondent delayed successive
25 17:13:12       subcontractors or the wider BMP schedule or Bapco
                                     203
 1 17:13:16       schedule.
 2 17:13:16           At the third level, the interfaces linked must be
 3 17:13:19       proved.
 4 17:13:19           Next slide, please.
 5 17:13:23           The causation themes in my assessment repeat
 6 17:13:28       across the lots -- things such as Danway handover,
 7 17:13:30       Banagas and EWA clashes, field engineering
 8 17:13:35       instructions and design changes, materials,
 9 17:13:38       dewatering, access issues, prerequisite works, and
10 17:13:42       also resequencing the works through obstructions.
11 17:13:45           This is not a single cause case.  It is a
12 17:13:48       cumulative sequence in which recurring interfaces and
13 17:13:51       constraints affect activities at particular times.
14 17:13:54           Next slide, please.
15 17:13:57           I then turn to disruption.
16 17:14:03           Delay and disruption are different questions.
17 17:14:06       Delay asks: what delayed the milestone?  And
18 17:14:08       disruption asks: what caused inefficient working,
19 17:14:10       reduced productivity or any additional man hours?
20 17:14:14           A work front can be disrupted even if some work
21 17:14:18       continued, and an event can cause disruption without
22 17:14:22       producing a neat day-for-day critical activity.
23 17:14:25           Next slide, please.
24 17:14:28           My disruption methodology uses DPR man hours and
25 17:14:36       WPR quantities -- that's daily progress reports and
                                     204
 1 17:14:39       weekly progress reports -- and productivity
 2 17:14:42       benchmarking and causation review, in accordance with
 3 17:14:45       industry best practices.
 4 17:14:46           I have used a measured mile analysis to assess 14
 5 17:14:49       categories of work.  The method is not a second
 6 17:14:53       critical path analysis.  It is an assessment of
 7 17:14:55       inefficient working and additional hours in the
 8 17:14:58       post-addendum 4 period.
 9 17:15:00           Next slide, please.
10 17:15:01           Mr Falkenstern places significant weight on work
11 17:15:08       front availability.
12 17:15:10           In my opinion, that argument is incomplete.
13 17:15:14           The question is not whether some other work front
14 17:15:16       existed somewhere on the project.  The question is
15 17:15:18       whether the work front was a genuine substitute for
16 17:15:21       the current delayed work fronts, with suitable
17 17:15:24       resources, suitable materials, plant permits,
18 17:15:28       handovers and sequence.
19 17:15:29           Otherwise, it is simply a mitigation argument, not
20 17:15:32       an answer to delay that has already occurred.
21 17:15:35           Next slide, please.
22 17:15:38           This slide shows why the available work front
23 17:15:43       point does not stand scrutiny.
24 17:15:45           The percentages relied upon by Mr Falkenstern do
25 17:15:48       not measure whether meaningful work front was actually
                                     205
 1 17:15:51       available to be worked on.  They are simply expressing
 2 17:15:53       the remaining foundations as a percentage of the
 3 17:15:56       remaining total.
 4 17:15:57           So when the table shows 100 per cent, that does
 5 17:16:00       not mean a complete or unconstrained work front
 6 17:16:03       existed.  It only means that 100 per cent of what
 7 17:16:05       remained at that point was still, theoretically,
 8 17:16:07       available.
 9 17:16:08           The important point here in the brackets, the
10 17:16:13       numerator progressively reduces over time, showing the
11 17:16:16       foundations were, in fact, being constructed and
12 17:16:18       closed out progressively.
13 17:16:20           For example, the apparent availability can remain
14 17:16:23       at or near 100 per cent, while the actual number of
15 17:16:26       the remaining foundations is actually falling
16 17:16:30       materially.
17 17:16:30           That is not evidence of abundant available work
18 17:16:34       fronts.  It is evidence of diminishing and
19 17:16:36       progressively consumed work fronts.
20 17:16:38           In my view, Mr Falkenstern's own table therefore
21 17:16:41       tells a slightly different story than the one that he
22 17:16:44       advances.
23 17:16:44           Next slide, please.
24 17:16:47           Mr Falkenstern also places reliance on CPI.  In my
25 17:16:54       opinion, CPI -- cost performance index -- may be a
                                     206
 1 17:16:57       useful warning light, but is not a reliable method of
 2 17:17:00       assessing critical delay.
 3 17:17:00           CPI measures cost efficiency.  It does not
 4 17:17:05       identify critical path, the delayed activity, the
 5 17:17:08       cause of delay or the number of critical days delay.
 6 17:17:11           That said, CPI is not irrelevant.  Where CPI
 7 17:17:17       deteriorates, it may indicate that the works were
 8 17:17:18       actually being carried out inefficiently and,
 9 17:17:20       therefore, it can support an assessment of disruption
10 17:17:23       when considered alongside productivity records, output
11 17:17:27       data, labour levels, access restrictions, work front
12 17:17:31       constraints and contemporaneous project records.
13 17:17:33           In other words, CPI may help show that the project
14 17:17:37       was becoming less efficient, but it does not tell us
15 17:17:40       whether the inefficiency caused critical delay.  It is
16 17:17:43       a warning light on the dashboard.
17 17:17:45           Next slide, please.
18 17:17:48           My principal criticism of Mr Falkenstern's
19 17:17:53       causation analysis is that large periods are left as a
20 17:17:57       balance of delay, low productivity or effective
21 17:18:00       suspension.  I do not dispute that the works were
22 17:18:02       delayed or that progress later became severely
23 17:18:05       constrained.
24 17:18:05           The point is that these are not descriptions of
25 17:18:10       the outcome.  They do not explain what caused
                                     207
 1 17:18:13       controlling activity to be delayed.
 2 17:18:15           Next slide, please.
 3 17:18:17           This slide addresses Mr Falkenstern's descoping
 4 17:18:21       and mitigation analysis.
 5 17:18:24           His position treats descoping as a significant
 6 17:18:26       mitigation event, because the removal of the work
 7 17:18:28       reduces the remaining scope.
 8 17:18:29           I do not disagree with that proposition.  At a
 9 17:18:33       general level, descoping can reduce the projected time
10 17:18:36       to completion, where the omitted work is on the
11 17:18:38       longest critical or longest planned path.
12 17:18:41           However, that general proposition does not answer
13 17:18:44       the retrospective delay question.  Descoping does not
14 17:18:47       remove critical delay that has already accumulated
15 17:18:50       before the scope is omitted.
16 17:18:52           At most, it may alter the contemporaneous critical
17 17:18:55       path going forward from the point of omission, having
18 17:18:59       little or no effect on the completion date.
19 17:19:00           My criticism is, therefore, that Mr Falkenstern
20 17:19:04       appears to treat the duration associated with the
21 17:19:07       omitted works as if it was automatically saved time.
22 17:19:10           In my opinion, that is insufficient.
23 17:19:12           The analysis must demonstrate the actual net
24 17:19:16       saving to the relevant addendum 4 milestone, having
25 17:19:19       regard for the controlling critical path at the date
                                     208
 1 17:19:22       of descoping and any subsequent path movement.
 2 17:19:25           The practical point is the theoretical reduction
 3 17:19:29       in scope is not the same as proven reduction in the
 4 17:19:32       sequence controlling completion.  That is the
 5 17:19:35       distinction I say is missing from Mr Falkenstern's
 6 17:19:38       approach.
 7 17:19:38           It should also be remembered that the most
 8 17:19:40       descoping was not the wholesale removal of that
 9 17:19:42       section of works, but simply the removal of the works
10 17:19:45       and reassigning them to another subcontractor.
11 17:19:48           As such, the works and the delay that was caused
12 17:19:51       had still occurred, and unless anything significantly
13 17:19:54       changed, then the descoped works would still present a
14 17:19:57       delay.
15 17:19:57           Next slide, please.
16 17:20:00           The second slide explains why a simple day-for-day
17 17:20:05       scoping calculation is misleading.  In a retrospective
18 17:20:08       analysis, removing an item of work does not
19 17:20:11       necessarily produce a day-for-day saving to
20 17:20:13       completion.  It simply exposes the next longest path.
21 17:20:16           For example, if the omitted work was on the
22 17:20:19       critical path, the next question is: what path became
23 17:20:22       critical once the work was removed?
24 17:20:24           If the next path is only slightly subcritical,
25 17:20:28       then the saving to completion may be very limited.
                                     209
 1 17:20:31       The saving is the net effect on the controlling path,
 2 17:20:34       not simply the duration or quantity of the work
 3 17:20:37       omitted.
 4 17:20:37           The test should therefore be, firstly, was the
 5 17:20:41       descoped work actually on the controlling path?
 6 17:20:44           Secondly, what was the next longest or near
 7 17:20:47       critical path at that date or point?
 8 17:20:49           Thirdly, had the descoped work already been
 9 17:20:52       delayed before omission?
10 17:20:53           Fourth, does the omission affect delay already
11 17:20:58       accumulated?
12 17:20:59           And, finally, what is the net saving to the
13 17:21:02       relevant addendum 4 milestone?
14 17:21:03           The second joint statement records my position
15 17:21:07       that the net saving to completion must be demonstrated
16 17:21:09       by reference to a controlling critical path and
17 17:21:13       possible path movement.  A simple equivalence between
18 17:21:15       omitted work duration and mitigation days is not
19 17:21:18       enough.
20 17:21:18           In my opinion, Mr Falkenstern does not undertake
21 17:21:21       that analysis in any sufficient detail.  The short
22 17:21:24       point is descoping may mitigate future delay, but it
23 17:21:28       is not a time machine.  It does not erase delay
24 17:21:31       already incurred, and it does not produce a mitigation
25 17:21:34       unless it is shown to reduce in the actual controlling
                                     210
 1 17:21:38       path to the relevant milestone.
 2 17:21:39           Next slide, please.
 3 17:21:41           I also do not accept the man hours or remaining
 4 17:21:48       durations can determine criticality by themselves.
 5 17:21:51       They may assist with scale and relative importance,
 6 17:21:54       but they do not prove the longest controlling sequence
 7 17:21:56       as described in the SCL Protocol.  A large work
 8 17:22:00       package may be non-critical.  A smaller prerequisite
 9 17:22:04       may control completion, however.
10 17:22:05           Next slide, please.
11 17:22:08           I should make it clear that I do not say that
12 17:22:12       every criticism made by Mr Falkenstern is not without
13 17:22:15       merit.  He's entitled to test over-attribution,
14 17:22:19       localised examples, and weekly evidence disruption
15 17:22:21       allocations.  That is part of the expert exercise.
16 17:22:24           My criticism, however, is that he goes too far
17 17:22:27       when he substitutes those criticisms with broad proxy
18 17:22:31       measures and residual explanations.
19 17:22:33           Next slide, please.
20 17:22:36           The conclusion I invite the tribunal to draw is
21 17:22:39       quite straightforward.  My assessment applies agreed
22 17:22:43       windows methodology to the post-addendum 4 period.  It
23 17:22:46       identifies activity based critical path, lot by lot,
24 17:22:49       and overall completion.  It separates delay from
25 17:22:53       disruption.  It also uses the claimant's own Bapco
                                     211
 1 17:22:56       reporting as consistency check on any wider allegation
 2 17:23:00       that the respondent controlled the main works delay.
 3 17:23:03           And my final slide, I close with these four
 4 17:23:11       points.
 5 17:23:11           Criticality is not proved by the largest remaining
 6 17:23:15       quantity of work or man hours.  Causation is not
 7 17:23:18       proved by low progress.  Descoping does not provide a
 8 17:23:23       day-for-day mitigation.  Both delay and disruption are
 9 17:23:27       not disproved by nominal work front availability.
10 17:23:30           My analysis identifies the controlling activities
11 17:23:33       in each window, and I have established criticality and
12 17:23:36       measured delay in accordance with the principles set
13 17:23:39       out in the SCL Protocol.
14 17:23:41           Furthermore, the claimant's own upstream reporting
15 17:23:45       to Bapco did not identify, as I understand, the
16 17:23:49       respondent's area 6 civils works as controlling the
17 17:23:51       main works critical path.
18 17:23:52           In my opinion, the better explanation remains the
19 17:23:56       activity based causation analysis in CM-1 qualified
20 17:24:00       and expanded in my second report and the joint
21 17:24:04       statement.
22 17:24:04           Thank you very much for your patience.

25 17:24:16       to come back tomorrow, 100 per cent, more refreshed
                                     212

 2 17:24:25       re-examination.
 3 17:24:26           Is that okay?
 4 17:24:29    MR BURKE:  I certainly hope so, sir.
 5 17:24:30    PRESIDENT:  Yeah.
